I guess the main reason I came in is that I’m just… tired all the time. And I know everyone says that, especially teachers, because by the end of the school day you’re answering a million questions and managing kids and then there’s grading and emails and planning, so I kept telling myself it was just that. But this feels different from the normal “teacher at the end of the day” tired.

Even when I get a full night’s sleep, I still wake up feeling like I have to force myself through the day. By the time I get home, it’s like I have to push myself through everything—grading, making dinner, getting things ready for the next morning. It’s not that I don’t know what needs to be done, it’s more like my body and mind are dragging through it all. I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve completely stopped liking them or anything. With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.

I guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting hard to just keep functioning like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like, like hobbies, time with friends, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve completely stopped liking them or anything. With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. But I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I used to love that now feels meaningless. It’s more blurry than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or walking around obviously miserable, so for a while I didn’t really think of it as “sad,” exactly. At school I can still smile at the kids, and I can do the lesson, and I can laugh if something funny happens. I’m not just sitting there blankly or anything.

But underneath that, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling. Like everything is muted a little. And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.

So… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.

By the time I get home, it’s like I have to push myself through everything—grading, making dinner, getting things ready for the next morning. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.

It started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.

At first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.

I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.

Like, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.

And sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.

So yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.

It’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.

But when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.

So no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.

That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting hard to just keep functioning like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like, like hobbies, time with friends, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve completely stopped liking them or anything. With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. But I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I used to love that now feels meaningless. It’s more blurry than that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down, sad, or in a low mood most days, even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or walking around obviously miserable, so for a while I didn’t really think of it as “sad,” exactly. At school I can still smile at the kids, and I can do the lesson, and I can laugh if something funny happens. I’m not just sitting there blankly or anything.\n\nBut underneath that, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling. Like everything is muted a little. And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you’re not really enjoying or getting pleasure from things you used to like, even when you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.\n\nAnd sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.\n\nSo yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself not caring about things you used to enjoy, to the point where it felt like they didn’t matter to you anymore, even when you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.

It’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.

With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. But I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I used to love that now feels meaningless. It’s more blurry than that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down, sad, or in a low mood most days, even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or walking around obviously miserable, so for a while I didn’t really think of it as “sad,” exactly. At school I can still smile at the kids, and I can do the lesson, and I can laugh if something funny happens. I’m not just sitting there blankly or anything.\n\nBut underneath that, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling. Like everything is muted a little. And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you’re not really enjoying or getting pleasure from things you used to like, even when you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.\n\nAnd sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.\n\nSo yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself not caring about things you used to enjoy, to the point where it felt like they didn’t matter to you anymore, even when you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even things you used to really like, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.\n\nIt’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d say I couldn’t feel any pleasure at all. I know we’re kind of circling around the same thing, but that wording—“any pleasure at all”—feels too absolute for me.

With teaching, for example, I don’t always feel the energy or satisfaction I think I used to, but there are still moments where a student understands something or says something sweet or ridiculous, and I do feel something. It might be small, and it might disappear quickly, but it’s there. And with painting or the garden, it’s similar. I might start out feeling kind of blank or disconnected, like I’m doing it because I know it’s supposed to be good for me, but then maybe I notice a color mixing nicely, or a plant that’s doing better than I expected, and there’s a little bit of pleasure or interest.

So no, I haven’t really felt like I’m completely incapable of pleasure. It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I came in is that I’m just… tired all the time. And I know everyone says that, especially teachers, because by the end of the school day you’re answering a million questions and managing kids and then there’s grading and emails and planning, so I kept telling myself it was just that. But this feels different from the normal “teacher at the end of the day” tired.\n\nEven when I get a full night’s sleep, I still wake up feeling like I have to force myself through the day. By the time I get home, it’s like I have to push myself through everything—grading, making dinner, getting things ready for the next morning. It’s not that I don’t know what needs to be done, it’s more like my body and mind are dragging through it all. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting hard to just keep functioning like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like, like hobbies, time with friends, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve completely stopped liking them or anything. With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. But I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I used to love that now feels meaningless. It’s more blurry than that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down, sad, or in a low mood most days, even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or walking around obviously miserable, so for a while I didn’t really think of it as “sad,” exactly. At school I can still smile at the kids, and I can do the lesson, and I can laugh if something funny happens. I’m not just sitting there blankly or anything.\n\nBut underneath that, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling. Like everything is muted a little. And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you’re not really enjoying or getting pleasure from things you used to like, even when you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.\n\nAnd sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.\n\nSo yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself not caring about things you used to enjoy, to the point where it felt like they didn’t matter to you anymore, even when you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even things you used to really like, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.\n\nIt’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t feel any pleasure at all, even in activities you used to really enjoy, like teaching, painting, or being in your garden?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I couldn’t feel any pleasure at all. I know we’re kind of circling around the same thing, but that wording—“any pleasure at all”—feels too absolute for me.\n\nWith teaching, for example, I don’t always feel the energy or satisfaction I think I used to, but there are still moments where a student understands something or says something sweet or ridiculous, and I do feel something. It might be small, and it might disappear quickly, but it’s there. And with painting or the garden, it’s similar. I might start out feeling kind of blank or disconnected, like I’m doing it because I know it’s supposed to be good for me, but then maybe I notice a color mixing nicely, or a plant that’s doing better than I expected, and there’s a little bit of pleasure or interest.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really felt like I’m completely incapable of pleasure. It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just the ones you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 35 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’ve thought about it in that broad a way—“almost all activities” makes me want to be careful, because I don’t think I could honestly say yes to that.

There have definitely been stretches where a lot of things feel less appealing, or I just don’t have that easy pull toward them. Like even ordinary things can feel kind of flat or not worth the effort in the moment. But I don’t know that it spreads to nearly everything. It’s more uneven than that. Some things might feel dull, and then something small still gets through, or I’ll find myself mildly interested in something I didn’t expect.

So… I don’t think I’ve had a time where I lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities. At least not in a clear, across-the-board way. It’s more like my interest gets dimmed or patchy, if that makes sense, rather than completely gone from everything.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I came in is that I’m just… tired all the time. And I know everyone says that, especially teachers, because by the end of the school day you’re answering a million questions and managing kids and then there’s grading and emails and planning, so I kept telling myself it was just that. But this feels different from the normal “teacher at the end of the day” tired.\n\nEven when I get a full night’s sleep, I still wake up feeling like I have to force myself through the day. By the time I get home, it’s like I have to push myself through everything—grading, making dinner, getting things ready for the next morning. It’s not that I don’t know what needs to be done, it’s more like my body and mind are dragging through it all. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting hard to just keep functioning like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like, like hobbies, time with friends, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve completely stopped liking them or anything. With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. But I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I used to love that now feels meaningless. It’s more blurry than that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down, sad, or in a low mood most days, even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or walking around obviously miserable, so for a while I didn’t really think of it as “sad,” exactly. At school I can still smile at the kids, and I can do the lesson, and I can laugh if something funny happens. I’m not just sitting there blankly or anything.\n\nBut underneath that, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling. Like everything is muted a little. And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you’re not really enjoying or getting pleasure from things you used to like, even when you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.\n\nAnd sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.\n\nSo yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself not caring about things you used to enjoy, to the point where it felt like they didn’t matter to you anymore, even when you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even things you used to really like, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.\n\nIt’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t feel any pleasure at all, even in activities you used to really enjoy, like teaching, painting, or being in your garden?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I couldn’t feel any pleasure at all. I know we’re kind of circling around the same thing, but that wording—“any pleasure at all”—feels too absolute for me.\n\nWith teaching, for example, I don’t always feel the energy or satisfaction I think I used to, but there are still moments where a student understands something or says something sweet or ridiculous, and I do feel something. It might be small, and it might disappear quickly, but it’s there. And with painting or the garden, it’s similar. I might start out feeling kind of blank or disconnected, like I’m doing it because I know it’s supposed to be good for me, but then maybe I notice a color mixing nicely, or a plant that’s doing better than I expected, and there’s a little bit of pleasure or interest.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really felt like I’m completely incapable of pleasure. It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just the ones you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it in that broad a way—“almost all activities” makes me want to be careful, because I don’t think I could honestly say yes to that.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where a lot of things feel less appealing, or I just don’t have that easy pull toward them. Like even ordinary things can feel kind of flat or not worth the effort in the moment. But I don’t know that it spreads to nearly everything. It’s more uneven than that. Some things might feel dull, and then something small still gets through, or I’ll find myself mildly interested in something I didn’t expect.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a time where I lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities. At least not in a clear, across-the-board way. It’s more like my interest gets dimmed or patchy, if that makes sense, rather than completely gone from everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Some things might feel dull, and then something small still gets through, or I’ll find myself mildly interested in something I didn’t expect.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a time where I lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities. At least not in a clear, across-the-board way. It’s more like my interest gets dimmed or patchy, if that makes sense, rather than completely gone from everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall a time when you genuinely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things that once brought you a sense of pleasure or fulfillment?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.\n\nIt’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t feel any pleasure at all, even in activities you used to really enjoy, like teaching, painting, or being in your garden?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I couldn’t feel any pleasure at all. I know we’re kind of circling around the same thing, but that wording—“any pleasure at all”—feels too absolute for me.\n\nWith teaching, for example, I don’t always feel the energy or satisfaction I think I used to, but there are still moments where a student understands something or says something sweet or ridiculous, and I do feel something. It might be small, and it might disappear quickly, but it’s there. And with painting or the garden, it’s similar. I might start out feeling kind of blank or disconnected, like I’m doing it because I know it’s supposed to be good for me, but then maybe I notice a color mixing nicely, or a plant that’s doing better than I expected, and there’s a little bit of pleasure or interest.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really felt like I’m completely incapable of pleasure. It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just the ones you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it in that broad a way—“almost all activities” makes me want to be careful, because I don’t think I could honestly say yes to that.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where a lot of things feel less appealing, or I just don’t have that easy pull toward them. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of a clear example, and honestly I’m not sure I can give you one in the way you’re asking. I’ve had times where I’ve been less drawn to things I normally like, definitely. Like I’ll look at my paints or think about going out into the garden and it feels more like effort than pleasure at first, or I’ll go through the motions and wonder why it doesn’t feel as satisfying as I expected.

But “genuinely lost interest” feels a little too strong, maybe. I don’t think I’ve had a clean break where something I used to love suddenly meant nothing to me. It’s more that I’ll feel distant from it, or the enjoyment feels kind of thin, and then there are still these little moments that remind me I do care about it. Like a color coming out right, or seeing something growing, or a moment at school where I remember why I like teaching.

So I guess my answer is… not really, not fully. I’ve had interest fade or get muffled, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost it.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I came in is that I’m just… tired all the time. And I know everyone says that, especially teachers, because by the end of the school day you’re answering a million questions and managing kids and then there’s grading and emails and planning, so I kept telling myself it was just that. But this feels different from the normal “teacher at the end of the day” tired.\n\nEven when I get a full night’s sleep, I still wake up feeling like I have to force myself through the day. By the time I get home, it’s like I have to push myself through everything—grading, making dinner, getting things ready for the next morning. It’s not that I don’t know what needs to be done, it’s more like my body and mind are dragging through it all. That’s what made me think maybe I should talk to someone, because it’s been getting hard to just keep functioning like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things that you used to like, like hobbies, time with friends, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I still do the things I usually do, and I don’t think I’ve completely stopped liking them or anything. With teaching, I still care about my students, and there are moments where something funny happens or a kid finally gets a concept and I do feel good about that.\n\nI guess I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Am I enjoying things less?” It’s more that by the time I have space for hobbies or seeing people, I’m kind of worn out, so it’s hard to tell whether I’m less interested or just… depleted. But I wouldn’t say there’s one specific thing I used to love that now feels meaningless. It’s more blurry than that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down, sad, or in a low mood most days, even when things are going okay?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or walking around obviously miserable, so for a while I didn’t really think of it as “sad,” exactly. At school I can still smile at the kids, and I can do the lesson, and I can laugh if something funny happens. I’m not just sitting there blankly or anything.\n\nBut underneath that, most days, there’s this kind of gray, flat feeling. Like everything is muted a little. And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you’re not really enjoying or getting pleasure from things you used to like, even when you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.\n\nAnd sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.\n\nSo yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself not caring about things you used to enjoy, to the point where it felt like they didn’t matter to you anymore, even when you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even things you used to really like, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m tired all the time, and it doesn’t feel like just the normal teacher-at-the-end-of-the-day tired.

I mean, teaching is tiring, of course, and I know everyone says that, especially with kids needing so much attention all day. But this feels different. Even if I get a full night’s sleep, I still wake up feeling like I’m starting the day already behind somehow. It’s not like I’m collapsing or anything dramatic. I can still do what I need to do. But it feels like I’m dragging myself through it, like everything takes more effort than it should, even after I’ve rested.

So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even things you used to really like, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.\n\nIt’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t feel any pleasure at all, even in activities you used to really enjoy, like teaching, painting, or being in your garden?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I couldn’t feel any pleasure at all. I know we’re kind of circling around the same thing, but that wording—“any pleasure at all”—feels too absolute for me.\n\nWith teaching, for example, I don’t always feel the energy or satisfaction I think I used to, but there are still moments where a student understands something or says something sweet or ridiculous, and I do feel something. It might be small, and it might disappear quickly, but it’s there. And with painting or the garden, it’s similar. I might start out feeling kind of blank or disconnected, like I’m doing it because I know it’s supposed to be good for me, but then maybe I notice a color mixing nicely, or a plant that’s doing better than I expected, and there’s a little bit of pleasure or interest.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really felt like I’m completely incapable of pleasure. It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just the ones you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it in that broad a way—“almost all activities” makes me want to be careful, because I don’t think I could honestly say yes to that.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where a lot of things feel less appealing, or I just don’t have that easy pull toward them. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I hesitate because “most of the day, nearly every day” sounds so exact, and I don’t want to overstate it like I’m miserable every single minute. I’m not. I can still function, and at school I can smile for my students and do the lesson and be warm with them. Sometimes I even sound cheerful, probably, because that’s part of what the kids need from me.

But underneath that, yes, there’s usually this gray, flat feeling that’s just sort of there. It’s like it follows me through the day and then really settles in when I get home. And even things that should feel comforting, like being in my garden or sitting down with my watercolors, can feel far away from me, like I’m looking at them through glass or something.

So if you’re asking whether it’s been a constant, heavy sadness every second, no, not exactly. But if you mean has that low, muted mood been there most days, as the background of my life, for a long time—then yes. It’s hard to admit, but yes, for at least the past couple of years.

And then when I get home, it follows me there. Even things that should feel like a relief—like going out to my garden or setting up my watercolors—feel far away from me somehow, like I’m looking at them through a window and can’t quite get to the part of myself that would normally enjoy them.\n\nSo… yes, I guess I would say I feel low most days, even when nothing is particularly wrong. It’s just there in the background.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you’re not really enjoying or getting pleasure from things you used to like, even when you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and a couple of months, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nIt started around the time my husband stopped working outside the home, and our whole household routine changed. I don’t mean that as blaming him exactly, because it wasn’t like there was one dramatic day where I woke up and everything felt different. It was more gradual. The rhythm of the house shifted, and the things I used to do that felt like mine—like painting a little, going out into the garden, just having some quiet time—started feeling harder to actually enjoy.\n\nAt first I thought it was just an adjustment period, like we were both getting used to a new normal. But then months went by, and then a year, and I realized I still felt kind of disconnected from things even when I was doing them. So… yeah, I’d say around two years and two months.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you genuinely felt pleasure or interest in something, even a small thing, and what was that like for you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and it’s harder than I wish it were. There are little moments, I think, but they’re kind of brief and easy to miss.\n\nLike, recently one of my students said something genuinely funny—not in a disruptive way, just this completely unexpected little comment—and I laughed before I had time to think about it. That felt real. For a few seconds I wasn’t evaluating myself or thinking, “Am I enjoying this?” I was just… there. And I remember feeling a little lighter afterward, almost surprised by it.\n\nAnd sometimes if I’m outside and I notice something in the garden—like a new shoot coming up or the light hitting the leaves in a pretty way—I do feel a small pull of interest. It’s not like the old feeling where I’d get absorbed and lose track of time, but it’s not nothing either. It’s more like a spark that doesn’t quite catch.\n\nSo yes, I can think of small moments. They just don’t seem to last very long, and they don’t always carry over into the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself not caring about things you used to enjoy, to the point where it felt like they didn’t matter to you anymore, even when you were doing them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it quite that strongly—like that they don’t matter to me anymore. That sounds almost… harsher than what it feels like.\n\nIt’s more that the caring is there, but it’s distant, if that makes sense. Like with my garden, I still know I care about it. I still notice if something needs watering, or if a plant looks like it’s struggling. And with painting, I still have this idea in my head that it’s something that belongs to me, something I used to really love. So I wouldn’t say I feel like those things are meaningless.\n\nBut when I’m actually doing them, sometimes it’s like I’m going through the motions and waiting for the feeling to show up. I’ll be holding the brush or standing outside pulling a weed, and I’ll think, “I should be enjoying this more than I am.” Not in a dramatic way, just… a quiet disappointment.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’ve stopped caring completely. It’s more like the connection to the caring is weaker or harder to reach than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy anything at all, even things you used to really like, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of absolutely nothing at all. I mean, there have been days where it feels very flat, where I’ll try something I normally like and it just doesn’t really land, and that can make the whole day feel kind of gray. But even then, if I’m being precise, I don’t think it’s ever been that I’m completely unable to enjoy anything even a little bit.\n\nIt’s more like the enjoyment is muted, or it takes more effort to notice. Like I might not feel excited about painting or gardening the way I used to, but then there’ll be some tiny moment—something pretty, or funny, or just peaceful—and I’ll feel a little something. It may not last, and it may not be enough to change my mood in any big way, but it’s there.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a total absence of enjoyment. More like the volume is turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t feel any pleasure at all, even in activities you used to really enjoy, like teaching, painting, or being in your garden?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I couldn’t feel any pleasure at all. I know we’re kind of circling around the same thing, but that wording—“any pleasure at all”—feels too absolute for me.\n\nWith teaching, for example, I don’t always feel the energy or satisfaction I think I used to, but there are still moments where a student understands something or says something sweet or ridiculous, and I do feel something. It might be small, and it might disappear quickly, but it’s there. And with painting or the garden, it’s similar. I might start out feeling kind of blank or disconnected, like I’m doing it because I know it’s supposed to be good for me, but then maybe I notice a color mixing nicely, or a plant that’s doing better than I expected, and there’s a little bit of pleasure or interest.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really felt like I’m completely incapable of pleasure. It’s more that the feeling is quieter than I want it to be, and I don’t trust it to last.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities, not just the ones you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it in that broad a way—“almost all activities” makes me want to be careful, because I don’t think I could honestly say yes to that.\n\nThere have definitely been stretches where a lot of things feel less appealing, or I just don’t have that easy pull toward them. Like even ordinary things can feel kind of flat or not worth the effort in the moment. But I don’t know that it spreads to nearly everything. It’s more uneven than that. Some things might feel dull, and then something small still gets through, or I’ll find myself mildly interested in something I didn’t expect.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had a time where I lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities. At least not in a clear, across-the-board way. It’s more like my interest gets dimmed or patchy, if that makes sense, rather than completely gone from everything.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you genuinely lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, even things that once brought you a sense of pleasure or fulfillment?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a clear example, and honestly I’m not sure I can give you one in the way you’re asking. I’ve had times where I’ve been less drawn to things I normally like, definitely. Like I’ll look at my paints or think about going out into the garden and it feels more like effort than pleasure at first, or I’ll go through the motions and wonder why it doesn’t feel as satisfying as I expected.\n\nBut “genuinely lost interest” feels a little too strong, maybe. I don’t think I’ve had a clean break where something I used to love suddenly meant nothing to me. It’s more that I’ll feel distant from it, or the enjoyment feels kind of thin, and then there are still these little moments that remind me I do care about it. Like a color coming out right, or seeing something growing, or a moment at school where I remember why I like teaching.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, not fully. I’ve had interest fade or get muffled, but I don’t think I’ve completely lost it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant loss of energy or feel fatigued even after resting, beyond just feeling tired from your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I’m tired all the time, and it doesn’t feel like just the normal teacher-at-the-end-of-the-day tired.\n\nI mean, teaching is tiring, of course, and I know everyone says that, especially with kids needing so much attention all day. But this feels different. Even if I get a full night’s sleep, I still wake up feeling like I’m starting the day already behind somehow. And then by the time I get home, I have to really push myself through things that shouldn’t feel that hard—grading, making dinner, getting my stuff ready for the next morning.\n\nIt’s not like I’m collapsing or anything dramatic. I can still do what I need to do. But it feels like I’m dragging myself through it, like everything takes more effort than it should, even after I’ve rested.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood has been present most of the day, nearly every day, for at least the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I hesitate because “most of the day, nearly every day” sounds so exact, and I don’t want to overstate it like I’m miserable every single minute. I’m not. I can still function, and at school I can smile for my students and do the lesson and be warm with them. Sometimes I even sound cheerful, probably, because that’s part of what the kids need from me.\n\nBut underneath that, yes, there’s usually this gray, flat feeling that’s just sort of there. It’s like it follows me through the day and then really settles in when I get home. And even things that should feel comforting, like being in my garden or sitting down with my watercolors, can feel far away from me, like I’m looking at them through glass or something.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether it’s been a constant, heavy sadness every second, no, not exactly. But if you mean has that low, muted mood been there most days, as the background of my life, for a long time—then yes. It’s hard to admit, but yes, for at least the past couple of years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. At least not in a way I’ve noticed as significant.

My appetite can vary a bit, but I think that’s pretty normal for me. Some school days I realize I didn’t eat much lunch because I was busy or distracted, and then other days I snack more when I get home, so it kind of evens out. I haven’t had a period where I was barely eating or where food seemed impossible or anything like that.

And with weight, I don’t think there’s been any major change. My clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone comment that I look like I’ve lost weight. I don’t weigh myself constantly, so I guess I could miss small changes, but no, I haven’t been losing weight without trying or anything that stood out to me.
